Perhaps this too was a disease, and but the reflex of the wild energy with which Hester had fought against her sorrows before Pearl's birth. It was certainly a doubtful charm, imparting a hard, metallic lustre to the child's character. She wanted — what some people want throughout life — a grief that should deeply touch her, and thus humanise and make her capable of sympathy. But there was time enough yet for little Pearl. 

她正要这么做时，阳光就消失了。或者，从珠儿那阳光的表情来看，母亲可能想象是孩子把阳光吸收了，等他们进入更阴暗的地方时再释放出来，以照亮道路。令她印象最深的是珠儿那永不衰竭的充沛精力，这让她感到，珠儿在其天性中有崭新而不可转移的活力。她没有像现在的很多孩子那样，同瘟病一起继承祖先的烦恼，患上忧郁症。也许这种活泼也是种病，恰恰反映出，在珠儿诞生前，赫斯特与悲伤做斗争时那种野性。这当然是种颇受质疑的魅力，为孩子的性格镀上了一层金属般坚硬的光泽。正如一些人毕生追求的那样，她希望能有一种忧伤来深深地触动她，让她更加人性，懂得同情。好在小珠儿还有的是时间。

"Come, my child!" said Hester, looking about her from the spot where Pearl had stood still in the sunshine. "We will sit down a little way within the wood, and rest ourselves."

“过来，我的孩子！”赫斯特说，一边环视着珠儿刚刚在阳光下站着不动的地方。“我们要在林子里坐一会，休息一下。”

"I am not aweary, mother, " replied the little girl. "But you may sit down, if you will tell me a story meanwhile."

“我不累，妈妈。”珠儿回答道。“不过，你要是能在其间给我讲个故事，倒是可以坐下来。”

"A story, child!" said Hester. "And about what?"

“那就讲故事，孩子！”赫斯特说。“讲什么呢？”

"Oh, a story about the Black Man, " answered Pearl, taking hold of her mother's gown, and looking up, half earnestly, half mischievously, into her face. "How he haunts this forest, and carries a book with him — a big, heavy book, with iron clasps; and how this ugly Black Man offers his book and an iron pen to everybody that meets him here among the trees; and they are to write their names with their own blood. And then he sets his mark on their bosoms! Didst thou ever meet the Black Man, mother?"

“噢，就讲那个黑男人的故事吧，”珠儿回答，拽着母亲的长外衣，仰着头，一本正经却又调皮地望着母亲的脸。“讲讲他常常在林子里做什么，为什么带着本书——那本又大又沉、还带着铁钩的书；讲讲这丑八怪黑男人，他是怎么把书和铁笔递给他在林子里遇到的每一个人，让他们用自己的血签上自己的名字。然后他还在他们胸前打上记号！你遇到过那个黑男人吗，妈妈？”

"And who told you this story, Pearl?" asked her mother, recognising a common superstition of the period. 

“谁告诉你这个故事的，珠儿？”赫斯特问道；她明白那是当时普遍的一种迷信说法。

"It was the old dame in the chimney-corner, at the house where you watched last night, " said the child." But she fancied me asleep while she was talking of it. She said that a thousand and a thousand people had met him here, and had written in his book, and have his mark on them. And that ugly-tempered lady, old Mistress Hibbins, was one. And, mother, the old dame said that this scarlet letter was the Black Man's mark on thee, and that it glows like a red flame when thou meetest him at midnight, here in the dark wood. Is it true, mother? And dost thou go to meet him in the night-time?"

“是炉角的那个老妈妈，就在你昨晚照看的那家。”孩子说。“不过她讲的时候以为我睡了。她说成千上万的人在这里遇到他，在书上写了名字，还在身上留下了记号。那个臭脾气的西宾斯老太太就是其中一个。妈妈，那老婆婆说，红字是黑男人在你身上留下的。午夜，你在这黑漆漆的林子里遇到他时，它像一团红火那样烧着。这是真的吗，妈妈？你是在夜里遇见他的吗？”

"Didst thou ever awake, and find thy mother gone?" asked Hester. 

“你有没有在夜里醒来，发现妈妈不见了？”赫斯特问。

"Not that I remember, " said the child. "If thou fearest to leave me in our cottage, thou mightest take me along with thee. I would very gladly go! But, mother, tell me now! Is there such a Black Man? And didst thou ever meet him? And is this his mark?"

“不记得有过，”孩子说，“你要是怕把我留在屋里，就应该把我带在身边嘛。我很乐意去的！不过，妈妈，现在就告诉我！有这样一个黑男人吗？你见过他吗？这个是他的记号吗？”

"Wilt thou let me be at peace, if I once tell thee?" asked her mother. 

“我要是告诉你，你能让我静一静吗？”她母亲问道。

"Yes, if thou tellest me all, " answered Pearl. 

“能啊，只要你都告诉我。”珠儿回答。

"Once in my life I met the Black Man!" said her mother. "This scarlet letter is his mark!"

“我遇到过黑男人一次！”她母亲说。“这红字就是他留下的！”

Thus conversing, they entered sufficiently deep into the wood to secure themselves from the observation of any casual passenger along the forest track. Here they sat down on a luxuriant heap of moss, which, at some epoch of the preceding century, had been a gigantic pine, with its roots and trunk in the darksome shade, and its head aloft in the upper atmosphere. It was a little dell where they had seated themselves, with a leaf-strewn bank rising gently on either side, and a brook flowing through the midst over a bed of fallen and drowned leaves. The trees impending over it had flung down great branches, from time to time, which choked up the current and compelled it to form eddies and black depths at some points, while, in its swifter and livelier passages, there appeared a channel way of pebbles, and brown, sparkling sand. Letting the eyes follow along the course of the stream, they could catch the reflected light from its water, at some short distance within the forest, but soon lost all traces of it amid the bewilderment of tree-trunks and underbrush, and here and there a huge rock covered over with grey lichens. All these giant trees and boulders of granite seemed intent on making a mystery of the course of this small brook; fearing, perhaps, that, with its never-ceasing loquacity, it should whisper tales out of the heart of the old forest whence it flowed, or mirror its revelations on the smooth surface of a pool. Continually, indeed, as it stole onward, the streamlet kept up a babble, kind, quiet, soothing, but melancholy, like the voice of a young child that was spending its infancy without playfulness, and knew not how to be merry among sad acquaintance and events of sombre hue. 

这样说着，她们已经进到了林子深处，深得绝不会被碰巧经过林子的路人看到。这会儿，她们在一处茂密的青苔上坐下来。在上个世纪的某个时期，这里长着棵巨松，根和枝干隐藏在浓荫里，树冠高耸入云。她们坐在一处山谷里，枝叶遍布两侧的缓坡，溪水从中流过，水底满是落叶。小溪上悬着的树木常年抛下的大树枝，阻碍了溪流，在一些地方形成了漩涡和深渊；畅通无阻的水道中，鹅卵石躺在河底，还有褐色的沙子闪闪发亮。他们顺着水流看去，可以看到林子不远处水面粼粼的反光，不过这反光很快就在错综复杂的树干，灌木丛，以及散布各处、披着灰色地衣的巨石间消失得无影无踪了。这些大树和花岗岩的砾石，似乎都给小溪披上了一层神秘的色彩。它们也许担心，这不停息的声响会悄悄地倾诉起这古老森林的心声，或是在平静的水面映出其隐藏的心事。确实，小溪一路悄悄地流淌着，水声潺潺，友好、安静、亲切却又忧郁，像是不能玩耍的婴儿，不知道怎样能在周遭悲伤的人们和阴郁的色调中快乐起来。

"O brook! O foolish and tiresome little brook!" cried Pearl, after listening awhile to its talk. "Why art thou so sad? Pluck up a spirit, and do not be all the time sighing and murmuring!"

“啊，小溪！啊，那傻傻的、讨人厌的小溪！”珠儿听了会儿溪水的轻吟，喊道。“你为什么那么伤心啊？打起精神，别总唉声叹气，叽叽咕咕的！”

But the brook, in the course of its little lifetime among the forest-trees, had gone through so solemn an experience that it could not help talking about it, and seemed to have nothing else to say. Pearl resembled the brook, inasmuch as the current of her life gushed from a wellspring as mysterious, and had flowed through scenes shadowed as heavily with gloom. But, unlike the little stream, she danced and sparkled, and prattled airily along her course. 

然而，在其短暂的林中时光里，小溪情不自禁地讲起了它经历的庄严时刻，况且看起来，它好像除此之外就没什么好讲的了。珠儿和小溪很像：她生命之流的源泉如小溪的一样神秘，流经的风景也投射着阴郁的暗影。但是和小溪不同的是，她一路舞动着，闪烁着，天马行空地什么都说。

"What does this sad little brook say, mother?" inquired she. 

“这伤心的小溪说了些什么呢，妈妈？”她问。

"If thou hadst a sorrow of thine own, the brook might tell thee of it, "answered her mother, " even as it is telling me of mine! But now, Pearl, I hear a footstep along the path, and the noise of one putting aside the branches. I would have thee betake thyself to play, and leave me to speak with him that comes yonder."

“要是你也有伤心事，小溪也许会告诉你，”她母亲回答，“甚至会像是在讲我的伤心事那样！不过，珠儿，这会儿我听到了沿路有脚步声，有人在拨开树丛。我希望你自己去玩，让我和过来的这个人讲会儿话。”

"Is it the Black Man?" asked Pearl. 

“那是黑男人吗？”珠儿问道。

"Wilt thou go and play, child?" repeated her mother. "But do not stray far into the wood. And take heed that thou come at my first call."

“你去玩会儿好吗，孩子？”她母亲重复道。“不过别在林子里走得太远。记着，我一叫你，你就回来。”

"Yes, mother, " answered Pearl. "But if it be the Black Man, wilt thou not let me stay a moment, and look at him, with his big book under his arm?"

“好的，妈妈。”珠儿答应道。“不过，如果是那夹着大书的黑男人的话，你可以让我待一会，看看他吗？”

"Go, silly child!" said her mother impatiently. "It is no Black Man! Thou canst see him now, through the trees. It is the minister!"

“去吧，傻孩子！”她母亲不耐烦地说。“那不是黑男人！你现在可以透过树丛看到他了。那是牧师啊！”

"And so it is!" said the child. "And, mother, he has his hand over his heart! Is it because, when the minister wrote his name in the book, the Black Man set his mark in that place? But why does he not wear it outside his bosom, as thou dost, mother?"

“原来是他！”孩子说。“妈妈，他把手捂在心口呢！是因为牧师把名字写在那书上，黑男人在他心口留了个记号吗？他为什么不像你那样，把标记戴在胸前呢，妈妈？”

"Go now, child, and thou shalt tease me as thou wilt another time, " cried Hester Prynne. "But do not stray far. Keep where thou canst hear the babble of the brook."

“现在快走吧，孩子，想给我捣蛋就下次吧。”赫斯特·普林喊道。“不过别走远。呆在听得到溪水声的地方。”

The child went singing away, following up the current of the brook, and striving to mingle a more lightsome cadence with its melancholy voice. But the little stream would not be comforted, and still kept telling its unintelligible secret of some very mournful mystery that had happened — or making a prophetic lamentation about something that was yet to happen — within the verge of the dismal forest. So Pearl, who had enough of shadow in her own little life, chose to break off all acquaintance with this repining brook. She set herself, therefore, to gathering violets and wood-anemones, and some scarlet columbines that she found growing in the crevices of a high rock. 

这孩子唱着歌走开了，沿着溪流而上，试着在忧郁的水声里融入轻快的旋律。但是小溪流还是得不到安慰，继续讲述在这阴郁的树林里发生的、悲伤而传奇的秘密，或是哀叹着即将发生的悲伤。这样，珠儿选择了与这哀怨的小溪绝交，毕竟她的小生命中已经满是阴霾了。于是，她去采集紫罗兰、木莲花，以及一些她在巨石缝中发现的红色耧斗菜。

When her elf-child had departed, Hester Prynne made a step or two towards the track that led through the forest, but still remained under the deep shadow of the trees. She beheld the minister advancing along the path, entirely alone, and leaning on a staff which he had cut by the wayside. He looked haggard and feeble, and betrayed a nerveless despondency in his air, which had never so remarkably characterised him in his walks about the settlement, nor in any other situation where he deemed himself liable to notice. Here it was woefully visible, in this intense seclusion of the forest, which, of itself, would have been a heavy trial to the spirits. There was a listlessness in his gait; as if he saw no reason for taking one step farther, nor felt any desire to do so, but would have been glad, could he be glad of anything, to fling himself down at the root of the nearest tree, and lie there passive, for evermore. The leaves might bestrew him and the soil gradually accumulate and form a little hillock over his frame, no matter whether there were life in it or no. Death was too definite an object to be wished for or avoided. 

那精灵似的孩子离开后，赫斯特·普林往延伸至树林的小路迈了一两步，不过还是呆在浓密的树荫底下。她看到牧师沿着小径走来，孤身一人，拄着一根在路边砍的手杖。他形容憔悴，神色消沉——这是他在居民区散步时，或是在任何他认为显眼的场合，都不会如此明显地表现出来的。这会儿，在这茂密幽闭的、让精神倍感压力的树林里，这神情如此明显，竟让人觉得悲凉。他无精打采地走着，仿佛不知为何要走下去，也不愿再走下去。如果还有什么可让他高兴的，那就是躺倒在最近的那棵树下，无所事事地躺一辈子。不管他是死是活，树叶会落满他全身，泥土会渐渐堆积，在他身上形成一座小山丘。死亡是必然的目的地，不必期盼或躲避。

To Hester's eye, the Reverend Mr. Dimmesdale exhibited no symptom of positive and vivacious suffering, except that, as little Pearl had remarked, he kept his hand over his heart. 

在赫斯特看来，除了像小珠儿提到的，他用手捂着心口，并没有显而易见的症状，表明丁梅斯代尔牧师先生正在忍受着痛苦。

# Chapter 17THE PASTOR AND HIS PARISHIONER

**第十七章 牧师和他的教民**

Slowly as the minister walked, he had almost gone by before Hester Prynne could gather voice enough to attract his observation. At length, she succeeded. 

尽管牧师走得很慢，可他几乎要走过去了，赫斯特还是提不起声音喊住他。终于，她喊了出来。

"Arthur Dimmesdale!" she said, faintly at first; then louder, but hoarsely. "Arthur Dimmesdale!"

“阿瑟·丁梅斯代尔！”她喊道，起初声音很弱，后来大了些声，却有些沙哑。“阿瑟·丁梅斯代尔！”

"Who speaks?" answered the minister. 

“谁在叫我？”牧师应道。

Gathering himself quickly up, he stood more erect, like a man taken by surprise in a mood to which he was reluctant to have witnesses. Throwing his eyes anxiously in the direction of the voice, he indistinctly beheld a form under the trees, clad in garments so sombre, and so little relieved from the grey twilight into which the clouded sky and the heavy foliage had darkened the noontide, that he knew not whether it were a woman or a shadow. It may be, that his pathway through life was haunted thus, by a spectre that had stolen out from among his thoughts. 

他迅速地打起精神，站得更直了，像是被吓到了——那种心情是他不愿被人看到的。他慌忙循声望去，隐约地看到树下有个身影。那人穿着暗淡，加之天空阴霾，树荫浓密，虽是正午却一片灰暗，如黄昏一般，因此他分不清那是个女人还是个影子。也许，在他的人生之路上，这意念里的幽灵常常会溜出来纠缠他吧。

He made a step nigher, and discovered the scarlet letter. 

他走近了一步，发现了那红字。

"Hester! Hester Prynne!" said he. "Is it thou? Art thou in life?"

“赫斯特！赫斯特·普林！”他说。“是你吗？你活着吗？”

"Even so!" she answered. "In such life as has been mine these seven years past! And thou, Arthur Dimmesdale, dost thou yet live?"

“还不止如此！”她回答。“过去的七年我都这样活着！你呢，阿瑟·丁梅斯代尔，你还活着吗？”

It was no wonder that they thus questioned one another's actual and bodily existence, and even doubted of their own. So strangely did they meet, in the dim wood, that it was like the first encounter, in the world beyond the grave, of two spirits who had been intimately connected in their former life, but now stood coldly shuddering, in mutual dread; as not yet familiar with their state, nor wonted to the companionship of disembodied beings. Each a ghost, and awe-stricken at the other ghost! They were awe-stricken likewise at themselves; because the crisis flung back to them their consciousness, and revealed to each heart its history and experience, as life never does, except at such breathless epochs. The soul beheld its features in the mirror of the passing moment. It was with fear, and tremulously, and, as it were, by a slow, reluctant necessity, that Arthur Dimmesdale put forth his hand, chill as death, and touched the chill hand of Hester Prynne. The grasp, cold as it was, took away what was dreariest in the interview. They now felt themselves, at least, inhabitants of the same sphere. 

他俩这样质疑对方肉体的实际存在，甚至怀疑自己的存在，是不足为奇的。他们在这幽暗的树林里离奇相遇，就像是两个幽灵出了坟墓后的首次相遇。他们在前世关系密切，此刻却冷冷地站着，瑟瑟发抖，吓坏了彼此。因为他们对自己现在的状况还不熟悉，也不习惯与这没有了肉体的存在为伴。他俩都是魂魄，还吓到了彼此！他们也被自己吓到了。因为这情急之刻唤醒了他们的意识，各自的心里都想起了那段历史和经历。除了这种令人窒息的时刻以外，那些事物从未在生命中发生过。灵魂在逝去的时光之镜中看到了自己的模样。阿瑟·丁梅斯代尔充满了恐惧。他周身颤抖，慢慢地伸出他死人般冰冷的手，可以说是有些勉强却是必须地，碰到了赫斯特发凉的手。这一握，即便冰冷，却驱走了会面中最阴沉的东西。至少，他们此刻感到来自同一天地了。

Without a word more spoken — neither he nor she assuming the guidance, but with an unexpressed consent — they glided back into the shadow of the woods whence Hester had emerged, and sat down on the heap of moss where she and Pearl had before been sitting. When they found voice to speak, it was at first only to utter remarks and inquiries such as any two acquaintances might have made, about the gloomy sky, the threatening storm, and, next, the health of each. Thus they went onward, not boldly, but step by step, into the themes that were brooding deepest in their hearts. So long estranged by fate and circumstances, they needed something slight and casual to run before, and throw open the doors of intercourse, so that their real thoughts might be led across the threshold. 

没有多说一个字，没有谁引路，只是凭着默契，他们退到了赫斯特刚才现身的树荫处，坐在刚刚她和珠儿坐过的青苔堆上。他们好不容易才开口说话；起先只是如两个熟人间的搭讪，谈那阴沉的天空、将临的暴风雨，还有各自的身体状况。他们这样说着，也不突兀，就慢慢地谈到了各自心中深藏的那个话题。由于长时间被命运和环境隔离，他们需要先聊些轻松随意的话题，打开交流之门，才能把真实的想法领进门槛。

After a while, the minister fixed his eyes on Hester Prynne's. 

过了会儿，牧师的目光盯住了赫斯特·普林的眼睛。

"Hester, " said he, "hast thou found peace?"

“赫斯特，”他说，“你得到安宁了吗？”

She smiled drearily, looking down upon her bosom. 

她苦笑了一下，低头望着自己的胸前。

"Hast thou?" she asked. 

“你呢？”她反问。

"None! — nothing but despair!" he answered. "What else could I look for, being what I am, and leading such a life as mine? Were I an atheist — a man devoid of conscience — a wretch with coarse and brutal instincts — I might have found peace, long ere now. Nay, I never should have lost it! But, as matters stand with my soul, whatever of good capacity there originally was in me, all of God's gifts that were the choicest have become the ministers of spiritual torment. Hester, I am most miserable!"

“没有！——只有绝望！”他回答。“像我这样，过着这样的生活，我能指望些什么呢？如果我不相信上帝，缺乏良知，是个本性粗野的恶棍，我或许早就得到安宁了。不能，我本不应该失去这些！不过，不论我的灵魂里原先有怎样优秀的品质，上帝所赋予我的那些最优秀的天赋，已经变成了精神折磨的执行者。赫斯特，我非常痛苦！”

"The people reverence thee, " said Hester. "And surely thou workest good among them! Doth this bring thee no comfort?"

“人们尊重你啊！”赫斯特说，“而且毫无疑问，你在他们中间做善事啊！这还不能带给你慰藉吗？”

"More misery, Hester! — only the more misery!" answered the clergyman, with a bitter smile. "As concerns the good which I may appear to do, I have no faith in it. It must needs be a delusion. What can a ruined soul, like mine, effect towards the redemption of other souls? — or a polluted soul towards their purification? And as for the people's reverence, would that it were turned to scorn and hatred! 

“更多的痛苦，赫斯特！只有更多的痛苦！”牧师苦笑着说。“我表面上在做善事，却没有一点信心。那是幻觉罢了。像我这样腐朽的灵魂，何以拯救其他的灵魂？或者，一个玷污的灵魂何以净化他人？至于人们的尊敬，让它们都化成轻蔑和愤懑好了！

Canst thou deem it, Hester, a consolation, that I must stand up in my pulpit and meet so many eyes turned upward to my face, as if the light of heaven were beaming from it! —must see my flock hungry for the truth, and listening to my words as if a tongue of Pentecost were speaking! — and then look inward, and discern the black reality of what they idolise? 

赫斯特，你觉得这算是慰藉吗？——我不得不站在布道坛上，迎着那么多双仰视的眼睛，仿佛我的面孔发散着天国之光！我不得不看着我那渴求真理的教民，倾听我的话语，如倾听圣灵在讲话！然后，我审视自己的内心，看到的是丑恶的真相，他们却顶礼膜拜！

I have laughed, in bitterness and agony of heart, at the contrast between what I seem and what I am! And Satan laughs at it!"

在内心的悲苦中，我嘲笑自己表里不一！撒旦也嘲笑它！”

"You wrong yourself in this, " said Hester, gently. "You have deeply and sorely repented. Your sin is left behind you, in the days long past. Your present life is not less holy, in very truth, than it seems in people's eyes. Is there no reality in the penitence thus sealed and witnessed by good works? And wherefore should it not bring you peace?"

“你这样就冤枉自己了。”赫斯特温和地说。“你已经深刻地忏悔过了。你的罪孽早已随岁月流逝了。你如今生活的圣洁，绝不亚于人们所看到的。那些善事不就实实在在地见证了你的悔过吗？这还不能带给你安宁吗？”

"No, Hester, no!" replied the clergyman. "There is no substance in it! It is cold and dead, and can do nothing for me! Of penance, I have had enough! Of penitence, there has been none! Else, I should long ago have thrown off these garments of mock holiness and have shown myself to mankind as they will see me at the judgment-seat. Happy are you, Hester, that wear the scarlet letter openly upon your bosom! Mine burns in secret! Thou little knowest what a relief it is, after the torment of a seven years' cheat, to look into an eye that recognises me for what I am! Had I one friend — or were it my worst enemy! — to whom, when sickened with the praises of all other men, I could daily betake myself and be known as the vilest of all sinners, methinks my soul might keep itself alive thereby. Even thus much of truth would save me! But, now, it is all falsehood! — all emptiness! — all death!" Hester Prynne looked into his face, but hesitated to speak. Yet, uttering his long-restrained emotions so vehemently as he did, his words here offered her the very point of circumstances in which to interpose what she came to say. She conquered her fears, and spoke. 

“不，赫斯特，不是这样的！”牧师回答。“这没有实质！它是冰冷死寂的，对我毫无用处！忏悔嘛，我已经足够多了！至于悔过，我却一点没有！否则，我早已丢下这身神圣的道袍，让他们看看，审判席上的我到底什么样子。你是幸福的，赫斯特，能公开地把红字戴在胸前！而我的红字却在暗中焚烧！你大概体会不到，经过七年欺己骗人的折磨之后，从一双眼睛里看到真实的自己，那是一种怎样的释然啊！我要是有这样一个朋友——即便是死敌也好！在我对人们的赞扬感到恶心时，可以随时去找他，并向他坦诚自己的罪大恶极，这样我想或许我的灵魂还可以得到拯救。即便这小小的真相就能挽救我！而如今，都是虚伪！都是空虚！都是死寂！”赫斯特望着他的脸，迟疑不语。不过，他这样热烈地吐露自己长期压抑的情感，倒是给了她机会，说出她此番的来意。她克服恐惧，开口了。

"Such a friend as thou hast even now wished for, " said she, "with whom to weep over thy sin, thou has in me, the partner of it!" —Again she hesitated, but brought out the words with an effort, "Thou hast long had such an enemy, and dwellest with him, under the same roof!"

“你现在想要的这种朋友，”她说，“可以让你哭诉自己罪过的朋友，不就是我嘛，我可是你的同伙呀！”她又犹豫了会儿，好不容易讲了下去：“你早就有那样的敌人了，还和他住在同一屋檐下！”

The minister started to his feet, gasping for breath, and clutching at his heart, as if he would have torn it out of his bosom. 

牧师站了起来，喘着粗气，拽着心口，像要把心掏出来一样。

"Ha! What sayest thou!" cried he. "An enemy! And under mine own roof! What mean you?"

“啊！你说什么！”他叫了出来。“一个敌人！和我在同一屋檐下！你什么意思？”

Hester Prynne was now fully sensible of the deep injury for which she was responsible to this unhappy man, in permitting him to lie for so many years, or, indeed, for a single moment, at the mercy of one whose purposes could not be other than malevolent. The very contiguity of his enemy, beneath whatever mask the latter might conceal himself, was enough to disturb the magnetic sphere of a being so sensitive as Arthur Dimmesdale. There had been a period when Hester was less alive to this consideration; or, perhaps, in the misanthropy of her own trouble, she left the minister to bear what she might picture to herself as a more tolerable doom. But of late, since the night of his vigil, all her sympathies towards him had been both softened and invigorated. She now read his heart more accurately. She doubted not that the continual presence of Roger Chillingworth — the secret poison of his malignity, infecting all the air about him — and his authorised interference, as a physician, with the minister's physical and spiritual infirmities — that these bad opportunities had been turned to a cruel purpose. By means of them, the sufferer's conscience had been kept in an irritated state, the tendency of which was, not to cure by wholesome pain, but to disorganise and corrupt his spiritual being. Its result, on earth, could hardly fail to be insanity, and, hereafter, that eternal alienation from the Good and True, of which madness is perhaps the earthly type. 

赫斯特·普林现在完全明白了这个痛苦的男人所受的伤有多深。对此她是有责任的，不该任由那个一心只有恶意的人摆布他那么多年——一刻也不该。那个心怀叵测的人不论戴上怎样的面具来遮掩，他的接近都足以干扰阿瑟·丁梅斯代尔那敏感的内心世界。有一段时间，赫斯特对此有欠考虑。或许，她自己痛不欲生，觉得牧师的厄运比较容易忍受，便弃之不顾了。但自从他夜游的那晚起，她对他的同情更加温柔而强烈了。现在她更能读懂他的心了。她毫不怀疑，罗杰·奇林沃斯的如影随形就像一剂暗下的毒药，毒化了他周围所有的空气。作为医生，他有权干涉牧师的身心疾病，而这些可乘之机却被恶意利用了。利用这些条件，他让病人的良心总是处在烦躁状态，这样下去，不但无助于治疗，还会扰乱他的精神状态。结果他终究会精神错乱，至此永远隔离于“真”和“善”之外，疯狂于尘世。

Such was the ruin to which she had brought the man, once — nay, why should we not speak it? — still so passionately loved! Hester felt that the sacrifice of the clergyman's good name, and death itself, as she had already told Roger Chillingworth, would have been infinitely preferable to the alternative which she had taken upon herself to choose. And now, rather than have had this grievous wrong to confess, she would gladly have lain down on the forest leaves and died there, at Arthur Dimmesdale's feet. 

这就是她带给这男人的毁灭，她曾经——唉，为什么不直说呢？即使现在她也热烈地爱着他！正如赫斯特对罗杰·奇林沃斯所说的，她觉得即使牧师以名誉和死亡本身为代价，也比她之前的选择要好得多。现在，与其承认这严重的错误，她宁愿躺在这林中的落叶之上，在丁梅斯代尔牧师的脚下死去。

"O Arthur, " cried she, "forgive me! In all things else, I have striven to be true! Truth was the one virtue which I might have held fast, and did hold fast, through all extremity; save when thy good — thy life — thy fame — were put in question! Then I consented to a deception. But a lie is never good, even though death threaten on the other side! Dost thou not see what I would say? That old man! — the physician! — he whom they call Roger Chillingworth! — he was my husband!"

“啊，阿瑟，”她叫道，“原谅我！在其他的事情上，我都努力地寻求真实！诚实是我可以坚守的美德，不论艰难，我也确实坚守住了它——除非你的利益、生命和名誉受到威胁！那时我才会同意欺骗。但即使面临死亡的威胁，谎言从来就是糟糕的。你难道不明白我所要说的吗？那个老人！那个医生！那个叫作罗杰·奇林沃斯的人！——他曾是我的丈夫！”

The minister looked at her, for an instant, with all that violence of passion, which — intermixed, in more shapes than one, with his higher, purer, softer qualities — was, in fact, the portion of him which the Devil claimed, and through which he sought to win the rest. Never was there a blacker or a fiercer frown than Hester now encountered. For the brief space that it lasted, it was a dark transfiguration. But his character had been so much enfeebled by suffering, that even its lower energies were incapable of more than a temporary struggle. He sank down on the ground, and buried his face in his hands. 

牧师像是被恶魔掌控了，狠狠地注视了她一会，试图压倒秉性中那些更高尚、更纯洁、更温柔的部分。赫斯特从未看过如此阴暗而凶狠的脸色。那神情停留了一小会儿，变化的过程十分骇人。但他的性格因苦难而变得软弱，那低劣品性的爆发也只能是短暂的挣扎。他瘫坐在地上，把脸埋在手中。

"I might have known it, " murmured he. "I did know it! Was not the secret told me, in the natural recoil of my heart, at the first sight of him, and as often as I have seen him since? Why did I not understand? O Hester Prynne, thou little, little knowest all the horror of this thing! And the shame!— the indelicacy! — the horrible ugliness of this exposure of a sick and guilty heart to the very eye that would gloat over it? Woman, woman, thou art accountable for this! I cannot forgive thee!"

“我本该知道的。”他低语道。“我其实是知道的！我第一眼见他时，心就畏缩起来，后来每每见他都是如此，这难道不是在暗示我什么吗？我怎么就不明白呢？啊，赫斯特·普林，你很难体会这事有多可怕！羞耻啊！卑劣啊！丑陋啊！这一颗病态而又罪孽深重的心，竟被这样暴露在那幸灾乐祸的眼皮子底下。女人啊，女人，你要对这事负责！我不能原谅你！”

"Thou shalt forgive me!" cried Hester, flinging herself on the fallen leaves beside him. "Let God punish! Thou shalt forgive!"

“你要原谅我！”赫斯特一边哭着，一边倒在他脚边的落叶上。“让上帝来惩罚我！你要原谅我！”

With sudden and desperate tenderness, she threw her arms around him, and pressed his head against her bosom; little caring though his cheek rested on the scarlet letter. He would have released himself, but strove in vain to do so. Hester would not set him free, lest he should look her sternly in the face. All the world had frowned on her — for seven long years had it frowned upon this lonely woman — and still she bore it all, nor ever once turned away her firm, sad eyes. Heaven, likewise, had frowned upon her, and she had not died. But the frown of this pale, weak, sinful, and sorrow-stricken man was what Hester could not bear and live! 

她伸出双臂搂住了他，突袭的柔情里满是绝望，把他的头靠在她胸前，不在意他的脸正好贴在红字上。他本想挣脱，却动弹不得。赫斯特不肯让他挣脱，害怕他会严厉地盯着她的面孔。长长的七年里，全世界都对这孤独的女人皱眉，但她还是挺过来了，从未转移过她坚定哀伤的眼睛。上天也曾向她皱眉头，而她活了下来。但这苍白的、无力的、负罪的、悲伤的男人一皱眉，赫斯特就无法忍受，活不下去了。

"Wilt thou yet forgive me?" she repeated, over and over again. "Wilt thou not frown? Wilt thou forgive?"

“你还能原谅我吗？”她一遍遍地重复着。“你能不皱眉吗？你能原谅我吗？”

"I do forgive you, Hester, " replied the minister, at length, with a deep utterance, out of an abyss of sadness, but no anger. "I freely forgive you now. May God forgive us both! We are not, Hester, the worst sinners in the world. There is one worse than even the polluted priest! That old man's revenge has been blacker than my sin. He has violated, in cold blood, the sanctity of a human heart. Thou and I, Hester, never did so!"

“我确实是原谅你了，赫斯特。”牧师终于回答了，悲伤地而不是气愤地深叹了口气。“我现在愿意原谅你了。愿上帝饶恕我们！我们不算是世间极恶的罪人。有一个人比受玷污的教士还要险恶！那老人的复仇比我的罪孽要深重。他冷血地亵渎了人心的圣洁。你和我，赫斯特，从没有做过这种事！”

"Never, never!" whispered she. "What we did had a consecration of its own. We felt it so! We said so to each other! Hast thou forgotten it?"

“从来没有，从来没有！”赫斯特悄声说道。“我们所做的是一种献身。我们这样认为！我们提到过的！你忘了吗？”

"Hush, Hester!" said Arthur Dimmesdale, rising from the ground. "No! I have not forgotten!"

“嘘，赫斯特！”阿瑟·丁梅斯代尔从地上站起来。“没有！我没有忘记！”

They sat down again, side by side, and hand clasped in hand, on the mossy trunk of the fallen tree. Life had never brought them a gloomier hour; it was the point whither their pathway had so long been tending, and darkening ever, as it stole along; and yet it enclosed a charm that made them linger upon it, and claim another, and another, and, after all, another moment. The forest was obscure around them, and creaked with a blast that was passing through it. The boughs were tossing heavily above their heads; while one solemn old tree groaned dolefully to another, as if telling the sad story of the pair that sat beneath, or constrained to forbode evil to come. 

他们又坐下了，肩并肩，手握手，坐在那根倒下的、长满青苔的树干上。这是他们生命中最阴郁的时刻。在前往这一刻的路途中，道路悄悄延伸，愈发黑暗。而这里却包含着一种魅力，让他们流连忘返，希望能再停留一会，再一会，还要再一会儿。周围的树林一片朦胧，风吹过，噼啪作响。树枝在他们头顶沉重地摇曳着。一颗肃穆的老树对另一棵树悲叹着，似乎是在讲述树下这一对人的凄惨故事，或是无奈地预告着行将到来的祸事。

And yet they lingered. How dreary looked the forest track that led backward to the settlement, where Hester Prynne must take up again the burden of her ignominy, and the minister the hollow mockery of his good name! So they lingered an instant longer. No golden light had ever been so precious as the gloom of this dark forest. Here, seen only by his eyes, the scarlet letter need not burn into the bosom of the fallen woman! Here, seen only by her eyes, Arthur Dimmesdale, false to God and man, might be, for one moment, true! 

而他们还是不肯离去。那回居民区的林中小道看上去多么沉闷！回到那，赫斯特就得重新担起屈辱之重荷，牧师就得戴上那虚伪的美名面具！于是他们又多逗留了一会。金色之光也不如这黑树林中的幽暗来得可贵。这里，只在他的眼前，红字才不会烧进这堕落女人的胸膛。这里，只在她的眼前，在上帝和人类面前虚伪的阿瑟·丁梅斯代尔，才可以有真实的一刻！

He started at a thought that suddenly occurred to him. 

他突然想到了什么，瞬时惊起。

"Hester, " cried he, "here is a new horror! Roger Chillingworth knows your purpose to reveal his true character. Will he continue, then, to keep our secret? What will now be the course of his revenge?"

“赫斯特，”他叫道，“有新的可怕之处呢！罗杰·奇林沃斯知道你有意揭露他真面目。他还会继续保守秘密吗？他现在又将怎么报复我们呢？”

"There is a strange secrecy in his nature, " replied Hester thoughtfully; "and it has grown upon him by the hidden practices of his revenge. I deem it not likely that he will betray the secret. He will doubtless seek other means of satiating his dark passion."

“他生性诡秘。”赫斯特若有所思地回答，“他暗中报复的行径更是发展了他这一秉性。我觉得他不太可能泄露秘密。他肯定会寻求其他办法来满足邪念。”

"And I! — how am I to live longer, breathing the same air with this deadly enemy?" exclaimed Arthur Dimmesdale, shrinking within himself, and pressing his hand nervously against his heart — a gesture that had grown involuntary with him. "Think for me, Hester! Thou art strong. Resolve for me!"

“可是我呢！和这死对头呼吸一样的空气，我怎么能活下去？”阿瑟·丁梅斯代尔惊呼着，缩着身子，慌忙地把头埋在手中——他这姿势早已是不由自主的了。“为我想想吧，赫斯特！你那么坚强。替我想想办法吧！”

"Thou must dwell no longer with this man, " said Hester, slowly and firmly. "Thy heart must be no longer under his evil eye!"

“你不能再和那男人住在一起了。”赫斯特坚定而缓慢地说道。“你的心不能再活在他恶毒的眼皮子底下！”

"It were far worse than death!" replied the minister. "But how to avoid it? What choice remains to me? Shall I lie down again on these withered leaves, where I cast myself when thou didst tell me what he was? Must I sink down here, and die at once?"

“这比死更可怕！”牧师回应道。“可是怎么避免呢？我还有什么选择呢？你告诉我他的真面目时，我就已经倒在了这枯叶上了，我还要再倒下去吗？我要这样倒下，马上死掉吗？”

"Alas, what a ruin has befallen thee!" said Hester, with the tears gushing into her eyes. "Wilt thou die for very weakness? There is no other cause!"

“哎呀，你都堕落成什么样子了！”赫斯特说，眼泪涌了上来。“你要死于懦弱吗？就是这样了！”

"The judgment of God is on me, " answered the conscience- stricken priest. "It is too mighty for me to struggle with!"

“我正受着上帝的审判。”良心受到震撼的牧师说，“它太强大了，我难以挣扎啊！”

"Heaven would show mercy, " rejoined Hester, "hadst thou but the strength to take advantage of it."

“只要你有抓住机会的力量，”赫斯特接口道，“上天会显示仁慈的。”

"Be thou strong for me!" answered he. "Advise me what to do."

“那你给我力量吧！”他回答道。“告诉我该怎么做。”

"Is the world, then, so narrow?" exclaimed Hester Prynne, fixing her deep eyes on the minister's, and instinctively exercising a magnetic power over a spirit so shattered and subdued that it could hardly hold itself erect. "Doth the universe lie within the compass of yonder town, which only a little time ago was but a leaf-strewn desert, as lonely as this around us? Whither leads yonder forest-track? Backward to the settlement, thou sayest! Yes; but onward, too! Deeper it goes, and deeper, into the wilderness, less plainly to be seen at every step, until, some few miles hence, the yellow leaves will show no vestige of the white man's tread. There thou art free! So brief a journey would bring thee from a world where thou hast been most wretched, to one where thou mayest still be happy! Is there not shade enough in all this boundless forest to hide thy heart from the gaze of Roger Chillingworth?"

“世界就这么狭小吗？”赫斯特深情地注视着牧师的眼睛，高声说道，像磁力一样，本能地作用在一颗涣散消沉的、颓废不振的心灵之上。“难道宇宙就只局限于那个小镇的范围吗？不久前，那里还是一片落叶遍地的荒野，就如我们现在周围一样凄凉？那林中小道通向哪里？你会说，是返回居民区的！没错，但也可能是通往前方的！它越往前去，就越是深入这荒野之地，脚下路就越是难辨。再走不远，枯黄的落叶上就见不到白人的足迹了。在那里你就自由了！这短短一程路，能带你走出那恼人的世界，去往一个仍能让你幸福的天地！这无边森林的浓荫，难道还不足以让你的心逃脱罗杰·奇林沃斯的窥视吗？”

"Yes, Hester; but only under the fallen leaves!" replied the minister, with a sad smile. 

“可以吧，赫斯特。不过只能在这落叶之下！”牧师苦笑一声，回答道。

"Then there is the broad pathway of the sea!" continued Hester. "It brought thee hither. If thou so choose, it will bear thee back again. In our native land, whether in some remote rural village or in vast London — or, surely, in Germany, in France, in pleasant Italy — thou wouldst be beyond his power and knowledge! And what hast thou to do with all these iron men, and their opinions? They have kept thy better part in bondage too long already!"

“那还有海上宽阔的航道啊！”赫斯特继续说。“是它带你来这儿的。你要是愿意，它还可以再带你回去。回到我们的故国，不论是偏僻的农村，还是大城市伦敦，或者去德国、法国，以及那令人愉悦的意大利，你就可以逃离他的掌控！到那时，这些铁石心肠的人和他们的想法，与你又有何干呢？他们已经把你善良的一面禁锢太久了！”

"It cannot be!" answered the minister, listening as if he were called upon to realise a dream. "I am powerless to go! Wretched and sinful as I am, I have had no other thought than to drag on my earthly existence in the sphere where Providence hath placed me. Lost as my own soul is, I would still do what I may for other human souls! I dare not quit my post, though an unfaithful sentinel, whose sure reward is death and dishonour, when his dreary watch shall come to an end!"

“不行的！”牧师答道，听着似乎是被召唤去实现一场梦。“我无力去那里啊！像我这样悲哀的罪人，除了希望能在这主安排我的地方苟活下去，就别无他求了。虽然我的灵魂迷失了，但我还是想尽自己所能拯救别的灵魂！我虽是个不忠实的哨兵，回馈我的必然是死亡和耻辱，我却也不敢擅离职守！”

"Thou art crushed under this seven years' weight of misery, " replied Hester, fervently resolved to buoy him up with her own energy. "But thou shalt leave it all behind thee! It shall not cumber thy steps as thou treadest along the forest-path; neither shalt thou freight the ship with it, if thou prefer to cross the sea. Leave this wreck and ruin here where it hath happened. Meddle no more with it! Begin all anew! Hast thou exhausted possibility in the failure of this one trial? Not so! The future is yet full of trial and success. There is happiness to be enjoyed! There is good to be done! Exchange this false life of thine for a true one. Be, if thy spirit summon thee to such a mission, the teacher and apostle of the red men. Or — as is more thy nature — be a scholar and a sage among the wisest and the most renowned of the cultivated world. Preach! Write! Act! Do anything, save to lie down and die! Give up this name of Arthur Dimmesdale, and make thyself another, and a high one, such as thou canst wear without fear or shame. Why shouldst thou tarry so much as one other day in the torments that have so gnawed into thy life! —that have made thee feeble to will and to do! — that will leave thee powerless even to repent! Up, and away!"

“你被这七年的苦难给压垮了。”赫斯特回答，热切地要用自己的能量给他鼓劲。“不过你应该把这些都抛到身后！这不应拖累你林中的脚步，要是你想穿越海洋，这也不应装入你的航船。把这残骸留在发生地吧。别与它纠缠了！一切都重新开始！就这一次失败，你就任何机会都没有了吗？不是这样的！未来还是充满机遇和成功的。还有幸福在等你享有！还有好事要做！把虚伪的人生换成一个真实的人生吧。要是精神在召唤，那就去完成使命，做红种印第安人的牧师和使徒吧。或者，顺从你的天性，去文明的世界，在那些圣人和名人中间做个学者和圣贤。去布道！去写作！去行动！做什么都好，只要不躺下死掉！抛掉阿瑟·丁梅斯代尔这个名字，换个名字，换个更高贵的、让你不会感到恐惧和耻辱的名字。你为何要这般，让痛苦一天天蚕食你的生命呢！它已经让你虚弱地无法期盼和行动！它甚至会让你失去后悔的力气！起来，离开这里！”

"O Hester!" cried Arthur Dimmesdale, in whose eyes a fitful light, kindled by her enthusiasm, flashed up and died away, "thou tellest of running a race to a man whose knees are tottering beneath him! I must die here! There is not the strength or courage left me to venture into the wide, strange, difficult world, alone!"

“哦，赫斯特！”阿瑟·丁梅斯代尔喊道，燃起了一束热情之光，可亮了一下又熄灭了，“你在让一个双膝颤抖的人去赛跑！我一定会死在这里！我已经没有力量和勇气去闯荡那广阔、陌生和艰难的世界了，何况还是独自一人！”

It was the last expression of the despondency of a broken spirit. He lacked energy to grasp the better fortune that seemed within his reach. 

这是一颗破碎的心满怀沮丧的最终表白。他缺乏力量去抓住那似乎唾手可得的美好未来。

He repeated the word. 

他又重复了那个字眼。

"Alone, Hester!"

“独自一人啊，赫斯特！”

"Thou shalt not go alone!" answered she, in a deep whisper. 

“你不会独自一人去的！”她郑重其事地低声回答。

Then, all was spoken! 

这样，话就全讲明了！

# Chapter 18A FLOOD OF SUNSHINE

**第十八章 倾泻的阳光**

Arthur Dimmesdale gazed into Hester's face with a look in which hope and joy shone out, indeed, but with fear betwixt them, and a kind of horror at her boldness, who had spoken what he vaguely hinted at, but dared not speak. 

阿瑟·丁梅斯代尔注视着赫斯特的脸，神情中散发着希望和欣喜，却也夹杂着畏惧，以及对她那勇气的惊恐。她说的他都曾暗示过，却不敢启齿。

But Hester Prynne, with a mind of native courage and activity, and for so long a period not merely estranged, but outlawed, from society, had habituated herself to such latitude of speculation as was altogether foreign to the clergyman. She had wandered, without rule or guidance, in a moral wilderness; as vast, as intricate and shadowy, as the untamed forest, amid the gloom of which they were now holding a colloquy that was to decide their fate. Her intellect and heart had their home, as it were, in desert places, where she roamed as freely as the wild Indian in his woods. For years past she had looked from this estranged point of view at human institutions, and whatever priests or legislators had established; criticising all with hardly more reverence than the Indian would feel for the clerical band, the judicial robe, the pillory, the gallows, the fireside, or the church. The tendency of her fate and fortunes had been to set her free. The scarlet letter was her passport into regions where other women dared not tread. Shame, Despair, Solitude! These had been her teachers — stern and wild ones — and they had made her strong, but taught her much amiss. 

而赫斯特·普林天生就头脑灵活，富有勇气，加上长时间疏远于世，为社会所抛弃，早已习惯了自由地思考。这于牧师是完全陌生的。她不受任何的约束和指引，流浪于道德的荒野。那荒野如这荒林般广阔、错综和阴暗，而此刻，正在这幽暗的荒林中，他们进行关乎命运的谈话。她的思想和心灵在这荒野之地适得其所，她像野蛮的印第安人一样在其林中自由徜徉。在过去的几年中，她就这样隔岸观火般地看待人类的风俗制度，以及由教士和立法者建立的一切。她几乎和印第安人一样，不屑于牧师的丝带、法官的黑袍、颈手枷、绞刑架、家庭或是教会。她的命运和前途总是通向自由的。红字是她进入其他妇女不敢涉足之地的通行证。耻辱，绝望，孤独！这些是她严厉粗暴的老师，成就了她的坚强，却也教会了她不按常理出牌。

The minister, on the other hand, had never gone through an experience calculated to lead him beyond the scope of generally received laws; although, in a single instance, he had so fearfully transgressed one of the most sacred of them. But this had been a sin of passion, not of principle, nor even purpose. Since that wretched epoch, he had watched, with morbid zeal and minuteness, not his acts — for those it was easy to arrange — but each breath of emotion, and his every thought. At the head of the social system, as the clergyman of that day stood, he was only the more trammelled by its regulations, its principles, and even its prejudices. As a priest, the framework of his order inevitably hemmed him in. As a man who had once sinned, but who kept his conscience all alive and painfully sensitive by the fretting of an unhealed wound, he might have been supposed safer within the line of virtue than if he had never sinned at all. 

而牧师呢，他从未有过一种经历，可以去引导他冲破社会准则，尽管他曾经冒犯了那最神圣的一条。但那是因冲动而犯下的，不是原则的对抗，也不是故意而为之。从那倒霉的时日起，他一直以病态的热情和谨慎监督自己。而被监督的不是行为——这很容易调整——而是丝丝缕缕的情绪和念头。如当年的牧师那样，身居高位的人只能更加受制于社会的戒律、原则，甚至偏见。作为牧师，他必然受等级观念的限制。作为一个罪人，他被未愈的创伤所困扰，良心不安，备受折磨，比起从未犯罪的他更加安分于道德的界限之内。

Thus, we seem to see that, as regarded Hester Prynne, the whole seven years of outlaw and ignominy had been little other than a preparation for this very hour. But Arthur Dimmesdale! Were such a man once more to fall, what plea could be urged in extenuation of his crime? None; unless it avail him somewhat, that he was broken down by long and exquisite suffering; that his mind was darkened and confused by the very remorse which harrowed it; that, between fleeing as an avowed criminal, and remaining as a hypocrite, conscience might find it hard to strike the balance; that it was human to avoid the peril of death and infamy, and the inscrutable machinations of an enemy; that, finally, to this poor pilgrim, on his dreary and desert path, faint, sick, miserable, there appeared a glimpse of human affection and sympathy, a new life, and a true one, in exchange for the heavy doom which he was now expiating. And be the stern and sad truth spoken, that the breach which guilt has once made into the human soul is never, in this mortal state, repaired. It may be watched and guarded, so that the enemy shall not force his way again into the citadel and might even, in his subsequent assaults, select some other avenue, in preference to that where he had formerly succeeded. But there is still the ruined wall, and, near it, the stealthy tread of the foe that would win over again his unforgotten triumph. 

这样我们似乎就明白了：就赫斯特而言，这备受摒弃和耻辱的整整七年，只不过是为这一刻做的准备。但阿瑟·丁梅斯代尔呢？他要是再次堕落的话，该用怎样的辩白来为他减轻罪责？没有了。除非这样解释，多少还有用：长期遭受的剧烈苦痛让他崩溃了；由于悔恨的折磨，他的头脑变得阴暗、混沌。在负罪而逃和继续留下做个伪君子的选择间，他良心很难找到平衡。他要躲避死亡和受辱的危险，还有敌人防不胜防的阴谋，这些都合情合理。这可怜的朝圣者在沉闷的大漠之路上，在虚弱悲戚之时，最终瞥见了充满人情味的仁爱和同情。那里有一个崭新且真实的生活，可以取代他正在赎罪的沉重命运。那严酷而悲戚的真理是，罪孽一旦在人类灵魂里留下裂痕，今世就难以弥合了。你尽可以防守，不让敌人闯入禁地，甚至可以迫使他在随后的袭击中选择其他途径，而非他曾经得逞过的方法。但那断壁残垣依旧存在，敌人在那附近暗中巡视，企图再次获得难忘的胜利。

The struggle, if there were one, need not be described. Let it suffice, that the clergyman resolved to flee, and not alone. 

那挣扎要是确实存在，便无须多说。这样说就足矣：牧师决定潜逃，而且不是独身一人。

"If, in all these past seven years, " thought he, "I could recall one instant of peace or hope, I would yet endure for the sake of that earnest of Heaven's mercy. But now — since I am irrevocably doomed — wherefore should I not snatch the solace allowed to the condemned culprit before his execution? Or, if this be the path to a better life, as Hester would persuade me, I surely give up no fairer prospect by pursuing it! Neither can I any longer live without her companionship; so powerful is she to sustain — so tender to soothe! O Thou to whom I dare not lift mine eyes, wilt Thou yet pardon me!"

“在这过去的七年里，”他想，“要是我能回忆起一瞬的安宁和希望，我就会为上天那仁慈的诚意而忍受下去。但如今，既然一切已命中注定不可挽回了，我为什么不去争取死刑犯临刑前的那点慰藉呢？或者，像赫斯特劝我的那样，如果这是一条康庄大道，我也不会因追求它而舍弃什么更好的前途。而且我的生活也不能再没有她相伴——她的支持如此有力，她的安慰如此温柔！啊，我不敢抬眼仰望的老天，你还能原谅我吗！”

"Thou wilt go!" said Hester, calmly, as he met her glance. 

“你会走的！”赫斯特说，眉眼中透着安详。

The decision once made, a glow of strange enjoyment threw its flickering brightness over the trouble of his breast. It was the exhilarating effect — upon a prisoner just escaped from the dungeon of his own heart — of breathing the wild, free atmosphere of an unredeemed, unchristianised, lawless region. His spirit rose, as it were, with a bound, and attained a nearer prospect of the sky, than throughout all the misery which had kept him grovelling on the earth. Of a deeply religious temperament, there was inevitably a tinge of the devotional in his mood. 

决定一下，一阵莫名的欣喜就如闪耀的光芒，投射到了他心中的那些烦恼上。这振奋人心的作用对一个刚刚摆脱心灵禁锢的人来说，如同踏上未被开垦的、不受基督教化的、不受法律管理的领地，呼吸着那里狂野、自由的气息。他的精神猛地升腾振作起来，比起被痛苦压倒在地那会儿，离天空更近了。他有着深刻的宗教情结，自然有一丝虔敬在心里。

"Do I feel joy again!" cried he, wondering at himself. "Methought the germ of it was dead in me! O Hester, thou art my better angel! I seem to have flung myself — sick, sin-stained, and sorrow-blackened — down upon these forest-leaves, and to have risen up all made anew, and with new powers to glorify Him that hath been merciful! This is already the better life! Why did we not find it sooner?"

“我又体会到快乐了！”他诧异地喊道。“我原以为快乐之源早已在我心中死去！啊，赫斯特，你是我的好天使！我似乎把那个病怏怏的、有罪的、伤心的自己扔在这林中落叶上，站起来时已经脱胎换骨，带着新生的力量来为仁慈的上帝添光彩！这就是更美好的生活啊！我们为什么不早一些想到呢？”

"Let us not look back, " answered Hester Prynne. "The past is gone! Wherefore should we linger upon it now? See! With this symbol, I undo it all, and make it as it had never been!"

“我们别回望从前了。”赫斯特回答，“过去的已经过去！为什么踟蹰不前呢？瞧！我取下这徽记，就像从来没有戴过一样。”

So speaking, she undid the clasp that fastened the scarlet letter, and, taking it from her bosom, threw it to a distance among the withered leaves. The mystic token alighted on the hither verge of the stream. With a hand's breadth farther flight it would have fallen into the water, and have given the little brook another woe to carry onward, besides the unintelligible tale which it still kept murmuring about. But there lay the embroidered letter, glittering like a lost jewel, which some ill-fated wanderer might pick up, and thenceforth be haunted by strange phantoms of guilt, sinkings of the heart, and unaccountable misfortune. 

这么说着，她解开扣着红字的别针，把它从胸口取下来，扔向稍远处的枯叶之中。这神秘的标志落在离溪流不远的地方。只消一手长的距离，这标志就要落入水中。若是那样，除了它此刻还在幽幽诉说的那个难解的故事外，小溪又承载起了另一段哀伤。但那刺绣的字就躺在那里，像一颗遗失的宝石，闪闪发亮。倒霉的流浪者或许会捡起它，从此被奇怪的罪恶之灵、沉沦的心灵和难解的厄运所萦绕。

The stigma gone, Hester heaved a long, deep sigh, in which the burden of shame and anguish departed from her spirit. Oh, exquisite relief! She had not known the weight until she felt the freedom! By another impulse, she took off the formal cap that confined her hair; and down it fell upon her shoulders, dark and rich, with at once a shadow and a light in its abundance, and imparting the charm of softness to her features. There played around her mouth, and beamed out of her eyes, a radiant and tender smile, that seemed gushing from the very heart of womanhood. A crimson flush was glowing on her cheek, that had been long so pale. Her sex, her youth, and the whole richness of her beauty, came back from what men call the irrevocable past, and clustered themselves, with her maiden hope and a happiness before unknown, within the magic circle of this hour. And, as if the gloom of the earth and sky had been but the effluence of these two mortal hearts, it vanished with their sorrow. All at once, as with a sudden smile of heaven, forth burst the sunshine, pouring a very flood into the obscure forest, gladdening each green leaf, transmuting the yellow fallen ones to gold, and gleaming adown the grey trunks of the solemn trees. The objects that had made a shadow hitherto embodied the brightness now. The course of the little brook might be traced by its merry gleam afar into the wood's heart of mystery, which had become a mystery of joy. 

赫斯特摘掉那耻辱的标记后，深深地长叹一口气。她的精神就此解脱了耻辱和苦闷的重荷。啊，多么美好的解脱！她感受到了自由，才明白那重荷的分量！又是一阵冲动，她取下那正儿八经的帽子。被束缚的头发垂落到肩上，乌黑丰盈，光影在浓密中闪现，为她的容貌颇添了一份柔美。她的嘴角和眼睛出散发着温和的微笑，那微笑好似涌自这女人的心头。她长期苍白的面颊，泛起阵阵红光。她的女性特质、青春气息，还有种种的美丽，从无法改变的过往中恢复了，带着少女般的期望和对未知的欣喜，汇集在此刻的魔力圈中。那天地的阴霾似乎是从两人心中流泻出来的，随着他们忧伤的消逝而消散了。上天在顷刻之间绽出了笑容，阳光四射，涌入这隐晦的森林，逗乐了绿叶，把枯黄的落叶染成金色一片，照耀在森森树木上，从它们灰色的枝干流泻下来。原先阴影的制造物，此刻如此光明。小溪伴着粼粼波光，欢快地溯源而上，进入树林的神秘心脏，那已成了欢快的神秘之地。

Such was the sympathy of Nature — that wild, heathen Nature of the forest, never subjugated by human law, nor illumined by higher truth — with the bliss of these two spirits! Love, whether newly born, or aroused from a death-like slumber, must always create a sunshine, filling the heart so full of radiance that it overflows upon the outward world. Had the forest still kept its gloom, it would have been bright in Hester's eyes, and bright in Arthur Dimmesdale's! 

这就是大自然对两颗狂喜心灵的同情和祝福。这蛮荒、异端的林中之自然，从不受制于人类之法，也不启迪于更高的真理。爱情，不论是新生的，还是从沉睡中苏醒的，都需要不时地制造些阳光，充盈那满是光辉的心灵，才能流泻世间。即便树林依旧灰暗，但在赫斯特和阿瑟·丁梅斯代尔的眼里，这里却是光明的！

Hester looked at him with the thrill of another joy. 

赫斯特望着他，心头又是一阵愉悦。

"Thou must know Pearl!" said she. "Our little Pearl! Thou hast seen her — yes, I know it! — but thou wilt see her now with other eyes. She is a strange child! I hardly comprehend her! But thou wilt love her dearly, as I do, and wilt advise me how to deal with her."

“你必须认识一下珠儿！”她说。“我们的小珠儿！你见过她——对，我确信！——但你现在会换种眼光来看她了。她是个奇怪的孩子！我简直无法理解她！不过你会像我那样深深地爱她，还要给我想想法子怎么对付她。”

"Dost thou think the child will be glad to know me?" asked the minister, somewhat uneasily. "I have long shrunk from children, because they often show a distrust — a backwardness to be familiar with me. I have even been afraid of little Pearl!"

“你觉得孩子会乐意认识我吗？”牧师有些不安地问。“长期以来我都躲着孩子，因为他们常常对我表示不信任，不愿和我亲近。我甚至害怕过小珠儿！”

"Ah, that was sad!" answered the mother. "But she will love thee dearly, and thou her. She is not far off. I will call her! Pearl! Pearl!"

“哎，那真是让人难过！”母亲应道。“不过她会深深地爱你的，你也会爱她的。她就在不远的地方。我叫她过来！珠儿！珠儿！”

"I see the child, " observed the minister. "Yonder she is, standing in a streak of sunshine, a good way off, on the other side of the brook. So thou thinkest the child will love me?"

“我看到孩子了。”牧师说。“她在那里，站在一束阳光下，离这儿有些远，在小溪的对岸。你觉得孩子会爱我吗？”

Hester smiled, and again called to Pearl, who was visible at some distance, as the minister had described her, like a bright-apparelled vision in a sunbeam, which fell down upon her through an arch of boughs. The ray quivered to and fro, making her figure dim or distinct — now like a real child, now like a child's spirit — as the splendour went and came again. She heard her mother's voice, and approached slowly through the forest. 

赫斯特笑了，又喊了声珠儿，看到她在一段距离之外，正如牧师描述的那样，宛如身披金色大衣的幻影，站在穿过拱形树枝的阳光里。那光线来回抖动，她那身影时明时暗，随着光线隐隐现现，一会分明是个活生生的孩子，一会像是孩子的精灵。她听到了母亲的声音，慢慢地穿过林子走来。

Pearl had not found the hour pass wearisomely while her mother sat talking with the clergyman. The great black forest — stern as it showed itself to those who brought the guilt and troubles of the world into its bosom — became the playmate of the lonely infant, as well as it knew how. Sombre as it was, it put on the kindest of its moods to welcome her. It offered her the partridge-berries, the growth of the preceding autumn, but ripening only in the spring, and now red as drops of blood upon the withered leaves. These Pearl gathered, and was pleased with their wild flavour. The small denizens of the wilderness hardly took pains to move out of her path. A partridge, indeed, with a brood of ten behind her, ran forward threatingly, but soon repented of her fierceness, and clucked to her young ones not to be afraid. A pigeon, alone on a low branch, allowed Pearl to come beneath, and uttered a sound as much of greeting as alarm. A squirrel from the lofty depths of his domestic tree, chattered either in anger or merriment — for a squirrel is such a choleric and humorous little personage, that it is hard to distinguish between his moods — so he chattered at the child, and flung down a nut upon her head. It was a last year's nut, and already gnawed by his sharp tooth. A fox, startled from his sleep by her light footstep on the leaves, looked inquisitively at Pearl, as doubting whether it were better to steal off, or renew his nap on the same spot. A wolf, it is said — but here the tale has surely lapsed into the improbable — came up, and smelt of Pearl's robe, and offered his savage head to be patted by her hand. The truth seems to be, however, that the mother-forest, and these wild things which it nourished, all recognised a kindred wildness in the human child. 

在她母亲坐着和牧师聊天那会儿，珠儿并不觉得时间难熬。这黑漆漆的大树林，虽然在那些把世间罪恶和烦恼尽藏于胸的人眼中是严肃的，对这孤独的孩子来说，不仅是个玩伴，而且知道怎么跟她玩。它是阴郁的，却抱着最佳的心情来迎接她。它送她红树浆果，那是上一个秋天长出来的，在春天才成熟，红得如血般滴在凋零的树叶上。珠儿采集了些浆果，很喜欢这山野的滋味。那些野生的小动物不会绕她而行。一只雌鹧鸪，身后跟着十只雏鸟，曾冲上来威胁她，但很快就后悔自己的凶蛮了，咯咯地示意她的孩子不必害怕。一只孤身栖息在低枝上的野鸽，让珠儿从它下面穿过，发出警告般的问候声。一只高枝作巢的松鼠，不知是生气还是高兴，对着那孩子叽叽咕咕叫了一番，还用松子砸她的头。松鼠这易怒却可爱的小家伙，真让人捉摸不透。这是去年的松子，早被它的尖牙咬过了。一只狐狸被她脚踩树叶的声音惊醒，好奇地打量着珠儿，似乎在犹豫着，是悄悄溜走好呢，还是继续在原地打瞌睡。据说——故事至此确实变得不太真实了——一只狼上前嗅了嗅珠儿的衣服，伸出自己充满野性的头让她拍。而真相大概是：森林母亲和它养育的这些野生动物们，都在这孩子身上察觉到了相似的野性。

And she was gentler here than in the grassy-margined streets of the settlement, or in her mother's cottage. The flowers appeared to know it; and one and another whispered as she passed, "Adorn thyself with me, thou beautiful child, adorn thyself with me!" — and, to please them, Pearl gathered the violets, and anemones, and columbines, and some twigs of the freshest green, which the old trees held down before her eyes. With these she decorated her hair, and her young waist, and became a nymph-child, or an infant dryad, or whatever else was in closest sympathy with the antique wood. In such guise had Pearl adorned herself, when she heard her mother's voice, and came slowly back. 

比起在两边铺着青草的居民区街道上，还有她母亲的小屋里，她在这儿可显得温柔多了。花儿似乎明白这点，在她经过时一个接一个地低语道：“用我来装扮你自己吧，漂亮的孩子，用我来装扮你吧！”为了让它们高兴，珠儿采了些紫罗兰、银莲花、耧斗菜，还有那从古树上垂到她眼前的翠绿嫩枝。她用这些装饰自己的头发和腰，变成了小仙女，或是林中精灵，或是任何与古老森林紧密联系的精灵。这样一番装束之后，珠儿就听见她母亲的召唤了，于是慢慢往回走去。

Slowly; for she saw the clergyman! 

她走得很慢，因为她看到了牧师！

# Chapter 1THE PRISON-DOOR

**第一章 监狱的门**

A throng of bearded men, in sadcoloured garments, and grey, steeple-crowned hats, intermixed with women, some wearing hoods and others bareheaded, was assembled in front of a wooden edifice, the door of which was heavily timbered with oak, and studded with iron spikes. 

一群蓄着胡须、身穿暗色外衣、头戴灰色尖顶高帽的男人，夹带了一些女人——她们有的蒙着兜帽，有的光着脑袋，都聚在一所木制的建筑物外，大门是用厚实的橡木制成，上面钉满了大铁钉。

The founders of a new colony, whatever Utopia of human virtue and happiness they might originally project, have invariably recognised it among their earliest practical necessities to allot a portion of the virgin soil as a cemetery, and another portion as the site of a prison. In accordance with this rule, it may safely be assumed that the forefathers of Boston had built the first prison-house somewhere in the vicinity of Cornhill, almost as seasonably as they marked out the first burial-ground, on Isaac Johnson's lot, and round about his grave, which subsequently became the nucleus of all the congregated sepulchres in the old churchyard of King's Chapel. Certain it is, that, some fifteen or twenty years after the settlement of the town, the wooden jail was already marked with weather-stains and other indications of age which gave a yet darker aspect to its beetle-browed and gloomy front. The rust on the ponderous iron-work of its oaken door looked more antique than anything else in the New World. Like all that pertains to crime, it seemed never to have known a youthful era. Before this ugly edifice, and between it and the wheel-track of the street, was a grassplot, much overgrown with burdock, pigweed, appleperu, and such unsightly vegetation, which evidently found something congenial in the soil that had so early borne the black flower of civilised society, a prison. But, on one side of the portal, and rooted almost at the threshold, was a wild rose-bush, covered, in this month of June, with its delicate gems, which might be imagined to offer their fragrance and fragile beauty to the prisoner as he went in, and to the condemned criminal as he came forth to his doom, in token that the deep heart of Nature could pity and be kind to him. 

新殖民地的建立者，不论他们起初对人类道德和幸福抱有怎样不切实际的理想，在其早期的实际需求中，总会意识到要划出一片处女地作为墓地，还有一部分要用来修建监狱。根据这个惯例，我们可以有把握地推断出，在波士顿定居的祖先们已经在康希尔一带修建了第一座监狱，同时期他们还在艾萨克‧约翰逊地带标出了第一块墓地。以坟墓为中心，皇室教堂的古冢便逐渐形成了。可以肯定的是，在城镇建立十五年或二十年之后，那座木制的监狱已经刻上了日晒雨淋和岁月流逝的痕迹，为它那狰狞阴森的大门增添几分晦暗凄凉的景象。笨重的橡木门上铁锈斑斑，显得比新大陆上的任何事物都古老。就像一切与罪恶有关的事物一样，这座监狱似乎从未经历过所谓的青春年华。在这座丑陋的建筑物前，一直到布满车辙的街道之间，是一片草地，长满了牛蒡、茨藜、假酸浆等此类难看的草木。它们显然与这片土地意气相投，因为这里很早便滋生了文明社会的罪恶之花——监狱。然而，在大门的一侧，几乎是在门坎处，长了一簇野玫瑰。在这个六月，玫瑰枝上开满了如宝石般精致的花朵。人们想象，它们会给踏进监狱的犯人，以及那些即将直面命运的刑徒，带来芬芳和妩媚。它们以此表示，大自然在内心深处仍对那些罪人怀有一丝同情与怜爱。

This rose-bush, by a strange chance, has been kept alive in history; but whether it had merely survived out of the stern old wilderness, so long after the fall of the gigantic pines and oaks that originally over-shadowed it— or whether, as there is fair authority for believing, it had sprung up under the footsteps of the sainted Ann Hutchinson, as she entered the prison-door — we shall not take upon us to determine. Finding it so directly on the threshold of our narrative, which is now about to issue from that inauspicious portal, we could hardly do otherwise than pluck one of its flowers, and present it to the reader. It may serve, let us hope, to symbolise some sweet moral blossom, that may be found along the track, or relieve the darkening close of a tale of human frailty and sorrow. 

由于某种不可思议的机缘，这簇玫瑰得以历劫而永生;仅是因为当年遮掩它的巨松和橡树倒下了，这簇玫瑰才得以在古老的荒野中存活下来吗？还是如人们所深信不疑的权威说法那样，当年圣徒安‧哈钦森踏进狱门时，玫瑰便从她脚下破土而出了呢？这都无需我们亲自去确定。既然故事要从这道不祥的大门开始，而这簇野玫瑰刚好就长在门坎处，我们不如摘下其中一朵玫瑰，将其献给读者。但愿这簇玫瑰，能在这个关于人性脆弱和哀伤的故事中，作为道德之花的象征，让人访得一丝甜美，或是在读完阴晦凄惨的故事结局时，使人得到些许慰藉。

# Chapter 19THE CHILD AT THE BROOK-SIDE

**第十九章 溪边的孩子**

Thou wilt love her dearly, " repeated Hester Prynne, as she and the minister sat watching little Pearl. "Dost thou not think her beautiful? And see with what natural skill she has made those simple flowers adorn her! Had she gathered pearls, and diamonds, and rubies, in the wood, they could not have become her better. She is a splendid child! But I know whose brow she has!"

“你会非常喜欢她的。”赫斯特·普林和牧师坐在一起望着小珠儿，重复道。“你不觉得她漂亮吗？看，她能用普通的花朵打扮自己，那真是生来的本事！就算她能在林子里采集到珍珠、钻石和红宝石，那些也不见得能把她点缀得更漂亮。她是个出色的孩子！但我清楚她的额头长得像谁！”

"Dost thou know, Hester, " said Arthur Dimmesdale, with an unquiet smile, "that this dear child, tripping about always at thy side, hath caused me many an alarm? Methought — O Hester, what a thought is that, and how terrible to dread it!— that my own features were partly repeated in her face, and so strikingly that the world might see them! But she is mostly thine!"

“你知道么，赫斯特，”阿瑟·丁梅斯代尔微笑着说，却有些不安，“这个总在你身边蹦蹦跳跳的可爱的孩子，曾有多么让我惊恐啊？我曾想——噢，赫斯特，那是怎样的念头啊，那种担心真是恐怖啊！——她的一些面容会与我惊人相似，世人兴许会认出来！不过她还是更像你！”

"No, no! Not mostly!" answered the mother, with a tender smile. "A little longer, and thou needest not be afraid to trace whose child she is. But how strangely beautiful she looks, with those wild flowers in her hair! It is as if one of the fairies, whom we left in our dear old England, had decked her out to meet us."

“不，不！不是更像我！”做母亲的温柔地笑着，回答说。“过不了多久，你就不用担心人们追究她是谁的孩子了。不过她头戴那些野花，看上去真是出奇地漂亮啊！就好像一位仙子，她被我们留在了亲爱的老英格兰，现在她把自己打扮起来了，来迎接我们。”

It was with a feeling which neither of them had ever before experienced, that they sat and watched Pearl's slow advance. In her was visible the tie that united them. She had been offered to the world, these seven years past, as the living hieroglyphic, in which was revealed the secret they so darkly sought to hide — all written in this symbol — all plainly manifest — had there been a prophet or magician skilled to read the character of flame! And Pearl was the oneness of their being. Be the foregone evil what it might, how could they doubt that their earthly lives and future destinies were conjoined, when they beheld at once the material union, and the spiritual idea, in whom they met, and were to dwell immortally together? Thoughts like these — and perhaps other thoughts, which they did not acknowledge or define — threw an awe about the child, as she came onward. 

他们坐在一块儿望着珠儿慢慢地走来，那种感觉是他俩都前所未有的。她身上联系两人的纽带依稀可见。这过去的七年，她像活生生的象形文字一样来到世上，揭示了他们竭力隐藏的秘密。要是有先知或法师能读懂那火焰般的文字，就会明白，一切都写在符号之中，显而易见。而珠儿就是他们生命的融合。不论先前有怎样的罪恶，一旦看到那肉体的结晶和精神的概念，看到那将永远共存的交汇体时，他们怎能怀疑他们前世今生的命运不是联系在一起的呢？这样的想法——也许还有一些他们没意识到的，或无法描述的想法——让那正走来的孩子显得令人敬畏。

"Let her see nothing strange — no passion nor eagerness — in thy way of accosting her, " whispered Hester. "Our Pearl is a fitful and fantastic little elf, sometimes. Especially, she is seldom tolerant of emotion, when she does not fully comprehend the why and wherefore. But the child hath strong affections! She loves me, and will love thee!"

“和她搭话时不要让她感到奇怪，不要太热情，也别太急切。”赫斯特低声说。“我们的珠儿有时精灵古怪。特别是，她在摸不着头脑时，受不了情感的流露。不过这孩子感情强烈！她爱我，而且也会爱你的！”

"Thou canst not think, " said the minister, glancing aside at Hester Prynne, "how my heart dreads this interview, and yearns for it! But, in truth, as I already told thee, children are not readily won to be familiar with me. They will not climb my knee, nor prattle in my ear, nor answer to my smile; but stand apart, and eye me strangely. Even little babes, when I take them in my arms, weep bitterly. Yet Pearl, twice in her little lifetime, hath been kind to me! The first time — thou knowest it well! The last was when thou ledst her with thee to the house of yonder stern old Governor."

“你难以想象，”牧师偏过头来看了眼赫斯特·普林，说道，“对这次见面，我是多么害怕，又是多么向往！不过说真的，像我对你说的那样，我不太容易赢得孩子的亲近。他们不会爬上我的膝头，不会在我耳边胡乱讲话，也不会回应我的微笑。他们只会离我远远的，奇怪地盯着我看。即便是小娃娃，我抱着他们，他们也会伤心地哭鼻子。不过珠儿才这么大，倒是有两次对我挺好的！第一次嘛，你很清楚！第二次就是你领她到那严肃的老总督家里去时。”

"And thou didst plead so bravely in her behalf and mine!" answered the mother. "I remember it; and so shall little Pearl. Fear nothing! She may be strange and shy at first, but will soon learn to love thee!"

“那次你为了我们母女进行了大胆的申辩。”做母亲的回答。“我记着呢，小珠儿也会记得的。别担心！她起初可能会感到陌生和害羞，但很快就能喜欢上你的！”

By this time Pearl had reached the margin of the brook and stood on the farther side, gazing silently at Hester and the clergyman, who still sat together on the mossy tree-trunk, waiting to receive her. Just where she had paused, the brook chanced to form a pool, so smooth and quiet that it reflected a perfect image of her little figure, with all the brilliant picturesqueness of her beauty, in its adornment of flowers and wreathed foliage, but more refined and spiritualised than the reality. This image, so nearly identical with the living Pearl, seemed to communicate somewhat of its own shadowy and intangible quality to the child herself. It was strange, the way in which Pearl stood, looking so steadfastly at them through the dim medium of the forest-gloom; herself, meanwhile, all glorified with a ray of sunshine that was attracted thitherward as by a certain sympathy. In the brook beneath stood another child — another and the same — with likewise its ray of golden light. Hester felt herself, in some indistinct and tantalising manner, estranged from Pearl; as if the child, in her lonely ramble through the forest, had strayed out of the sphere in which she and her mother dwelt together, and was now vainly seeking to return to it. 

这会儿珠儿已经到了小溪对岸，一声不响地望着赫斯特和牧师。他俩依旧一起坐在长满青苔的树干上，等着见她。就在她停下的那个地方，小溪恰好形成了一个水塘，水面平静而光滑；在花朵和树叶编成的装饰下，她那如画的美丽倒映其中，玲珑别致，比她本人更精致而富有灵性。这倒影像极了珠儿本人，仿佛向她传达着自己那阴影般莫测的品性。珠儿站的姿态有些怪，她透过林荫紧紧地盯着他们，同时一束阳光像是受了某种感应，令她光辉四射。她脚下的小溪里，站着另一个孩子，另一个一模一样的孩子，身上同样是金光熠熠。赫斯特莫名而痛心地感到，自己与珠儿变得陌生起来，仿佛那孩子一个人在树林里游荡着，竟走出了她们母女共同生活的界线，努力地想回来却总是徒劳。

There was both truth and error in the impression; the child and mother were estranged, but through Hester's fault, not Pearl's. Since the latter rambled from her side, another inmate had been admitted within the circle of the mother's feelings, and so modified the aspect of them all, that Pearl, the returning wanderer, could not find her wonted place, and hardly knew where she was. 

这感觉几分是真，几分是假。这母女是疏远了些，但原因在于赫斯特，而不是珠儿。自从孩子离开母亲那会儿，另一个亲人来到母亲的感情圈内，从而改变了三人的地位。珠儿这个回来的流浪儿，找不到她一向的位置，几乎不知道自己身在何方了。

"I have a strange fancy, " observed the sensitive minister, "that this brook is the boundary between two worlds, and that thou canst never meet thy Pearl again. Or is she an elfish spirit, who, as the legends of our childhood taught us, is forbidden to cross a running stream? Pray hasten her; for this delay has already imparted a tremor to my nerves."

“我有个离奇的想法，”敏感的牧师观察到，“这小溪是两个世界的边界，你再也见不到珠儿了。或者她是我们儿时故事里的精灵，不被允许跨过这流淌的溪流？请快催催她，她迟迟不现身，都让我神经发抖了。”

"Come, dearest child!" said Hester encouragingly, and stretching out both her arms. "How slow thou art! When hast thou been so sluggish before now? Here is a friend of mine, who must be thy friend also. Thou wilt have twice as much love, henceforward, as thy mother alone could give thee! Leap across the brook and come to us. Thou canst leap like a young deer!"

“过来，亲爱的宝贝！”赫斯特伸出双手，带着鼓励的口吻。“你真是慢啊！你什么时候像现在这么懒洋洋的了？这里有我的一个朋友，也会成为你的朋友的。从现在起，你不只有妈妈一个人的爱了，你会得到双倍的爱！跳过那小溪，过来吧。你不是跳得像一头小鹿嘛！”

Pearl, without responding in any manner to these honey-sweet expressions, remained on the other side of the brook. Now she fixed her bright, wild eyes on her mother, now on the minister, and now included them both in the same glance, as if to detect and explain to herself the relation which they bore to one another. For some unaccountable reason, as Arthur Dimmesdale felt the child's eyes upon himself, his hand — with that gesture so habitual as to have become involuntary — stole over his heart. At length, assuming a singular air of authority, Pearl stretched out her hand, with the small forefinger extended, and pointing evidently towards her mother's breast. And beneath, in the mirror of the brook, there was the flower-girdled and sunny image of little Pearl, pointing her small forefinger too. 

珠儿呆在对岸，对这甜蜜的话语不作一丝回应。她时而用明亮而狂野的眼睛盯着妈妈看，时而又盯着牧师看，时而同时盯着他们两个看，似乎要弄清并向自己解释两人的关系。阿瑟·丁梅斯代尔莫名地感到孩子正盯着自己，手不自觉地因习惯而悄悄捂住了心口。终于，珠儿作出一副独特的不容置辩的表情，伸出那小小的手指，显然是指向她妈妈的胸部。脚下，镜子般的水面倒映着小珠儿的身影，她戴着花环，身披阳光，同样伸着那小小的食指。

"Thou strange child, why dost thou not come to me?" exclaimed Hester. 

“你这奇怪的孩子，怎么不过来呢？”赫斯特叫道。

Pearl still pointed with her forefinger; and a frown gathered on her brow, the more impressive from the childish, the almost baby-like aspect of the features that conveyed it. As her mother still kept beckoning to her and arraying her face in a holiday suit of unaccustomed smiles, the child stamped her foot with a yet more imperious look and gesture. In the brook, again, was the fantastic beauty of the image, with its reflected frown, its pointed finger, and imperious gesture, giving emphasis to the aspect of little Pearl. 

珠儿还是伸着食指，皱起眉头，那孩子气的、婴儿般的姿态，让人印象尤深。她母亲还是在呼唤她，脸上露出不同以往的笑容，那孩子就摆出更加专横的神情和姿态，跺起了脚来。小溪里面依旧是那美得出奇的倒影，皱着眉头，伸着手指，姿势专横，为小珠儿平添了几分模样。

"Hasten, Pearl; or I shall be angry with thee!" cried Hester Prynne, who, however inured to such behaviour on the elf-child's part at other seasons, was naturally anxious for a more seemly deportment now. "Leap across the brook, naughty child, and run hither! Else I must come to thee!"

“快啊，珠儿，不然我可要生气了！”赫斯特喊道。无论她平时多么习惯这孩子的古灵精怪，但此时自然巴不得她能规矩些。“跳过那小溪，调皮鬼，跑过来！不然我就过去了！”

But Pearl, not a whit startled at her mother's threats any more than mollified by her entreaties, now suddenly burst into a fit of passion, gesticulating violently, and throwing her small figure into the most extravagant contortions. She accompanied this wild outbreak with piercing shrieks, which the woods reverberated on all sides; so that, alone as she was in her childish and unreasonable wrath, it seemed as if a hidden multitude were lending her their sympathy and encouragement. Seen in the brook, once more, was the shadowy wraith of Pearl's image, crowned and girdled with flowers, but stamping its foot, wildly gesticulating, and, in the midst of all, still pointing its small forefinger at Hester's bosom! 

可珠儿似乎对母亲的恳求无动于衷，她一点也没有被母亲的威胁吓到。这会儿，她突然闹脾气了，作出凶狠的姿态，夸张地扭动着小小的身子。她的爆发还伴着尖叫，声音在树林四处回响。这样，她虽是一个人乱发孩子脾气，却好像有许多隐藏着的人在同情她，鼓励她。溪水中还是可以看到珠儿的倒影，头戴花冠，腰缠花带，跺着脚，暴躁地做着动作，同时，还是用食指指着赫斯特的胸部！

"I see what ails the child, " whispered Hester to the clergyman, and turning pale in spite of a strong effort to conceal her trouble and annoyance. "Children will not abide any, the slightest, change in the accustomed aspect of things that are daily before their eyes. Pearl misses something which she has always seen me wear."

“我知道是什么在烦扰孩子了，”赫斯特低声对牧师说道。她虽然努力掩饰着自己的困扰，却还是面色发白。“孩子不能容忍那些习以为常的事物有任何轻微的改变。珠儿看不到我那不离身的徽记了。”

"I pray you, " answered the minister, "if thou hast any means of pacifying the child, do it forthwith! Save it were the cankered wrath of an old witch, like Mistress Hibbins, " added he, attempting to smile, "I know nothing that I would not sooner encounter than this passion in a child. In Pearl's young beauty, as in the wrinkled witch, it has a preternatural effect. Pacify her, if thou lovest me!"

“我求求你，”牧师应道，“要是有什么办法能让孩子平静下来，就马上行动吧！只要不是老妖婆式的发怒，像西宾斯太太那样。”他强笑着补充说，“再也没有比这孩子发脾气更不愿让人见到的了。年轻漂亮的珠儿和那满是皱纹的老妖婆一样，身上有种超自然的力量。让她安静下来吧，如果你爱我！”

Hester turned again towards Pearl with a crimson blush upon her cheek, a conscious glance aside at the clergyman, and then a heavy sigh; while, even before she had time to speak, the blush yielded to a deadly pallor. 

赫斯特又转向珠儿，面颊绯红，下意识地斜睨了眼牧师，深深地叹了口气。她还没来得及说话，绯红的脸已经变得苍白了。

"Pearl, " said she, sadly, "look down at thy feet! There — before thee! — the hither side of the brook!"

“珠儿，”她说，“看看你脚下！那里，就在你跟前！——小溪的另一边！”

The child turned her eyes to the point indicated; and there lay the scarlet letter, so close upon the margin of the stream that the gold embroidery was reflected in it. 

孩子把目光转向所指的地方——红字就躺在那里，紧靠着岸边，那金刺绣的光反射在水面上。

"Bring it hither!" said Hester. 

“把它捡回来！”赫斯特说。

"Come thou and take it up!" answered Pearl. 

“你过来捡吧！”珠儿回答。

"Was ever such a child!" observed Hester, aside to the minister. "Oh, I have much to tell thee about her! But, in very truth, she is right as regards this hateful token. I must bear its torture yet a little longer — only a few days longer — until we shall have left this region and look back hither as to a land which we have dreamed of. The forest cannot hide it! The mid-ocean shall take it from my hand, and swallow it up forever!"

“哪有这样的孩子！”赫斯特对一旁的牧师说道。“噢，我有很多关于她的事情要告诉你！不过，关于这徽记的事她确实没有错。我还得多忍受会儿这折磨人的玩意儿——几天就好——直到我们离开这地方。等我们再回头看时，这就只是一块曾经的梦想之地了。这树林藏不住它！大海可以从我手里带走它，永久地把它吞没！”

With these words, she advanced to the margin of the brook, took up the scarlet letter, and fastened it again into her bosom. Hopefully, but a moment ago, as Hester had spoken of drowning it in the deep sea, there was a sense of inevitable doom upon her, as she thus received back this deadly symbol from the hand of fate. She had flung it into infinite space! — she had drawn an hour's free breath! — and here again was the scarlet misery, glittering on the old spot! So it ever is, whether thus typified or no, that an evil deed invests itself with the character of doom. Hester next gathered up the heavy tresses of her hair, and confined them beneath her cap. As if there were a withering spell in the sad letter, her beauty, the warmth and richness of her womanhood, departed, like fading sunshine; and a grey shadow seemed to fall across her. 

说完这些，她走到小溪边上，捡起那红字，把它重新戴在胸前。片刻之前，赫斯特还满怀希望地说要把红字沉到海底，可当她从命运之手中接过那致命的徽记时，她感到了一种难逃之劫。她已经把它抛向了无限的空间！她呼吸了一小时的自由空气！这会儿，那红色的痛苦又回来了，在老地方闪闪发光！罪恶的行为不管有否表征，总是厄运在身的。于是，赫斯特挽起那浓密的头发，用帽子盖了起来。那哀伤的字母像是被下了诅咒，她的美丽，和那女性特质的丰富和温情，如落日般褪去了，仿佛有一抹灰影落在她身上。

When the dreary change was wrought, she extended her hand to Pearl. 

在这阴郁的变化后，她向珠儿伸出了手。

"Dost thou know thy mother now, child?" asked she, reproachfully, but with a subdued tone. "Wilt thou come across the brook and own thy mother, now that she has her shame upon her — now that she is sad?"

“你现在认识妈妈了吗，孩子？”她有些责备地问道，却克制着自己的语气。“妈妈又戴上了她的耻辱，妈妈又伤心了，你现在可以跨过小溪认你妈妈了吧？”

"Yes; now I will!" answered the child, bounding across the brook, and clasping Hester in her arms. "Now thou art my mother indeed! And I am thy little Pearl!"

“好的，我过来了！”孩子说着，跳过了小溪，搂住了赫斯特。“现在你真的是我妈妈了！我是你的小珠儿！”

In a mood of tenderness that was not usual with her, she drew down her mother's head, and kissed her brow and both her cheeks. But then — by a kind of necessity that always impelled this child to alloy whatever comfort she might chance to give with a throb of anguish — Pearl put up her mouth, and kissed the scarlet letter too! 

她拽着母亲的头，用一种不常有的温柔，亲了亲她的额头和双颊。不过，在偶尔给人的欣慰中，这孩子总免不了融进一阵苦恼。她撅起嘴，也亲了亲那红字！

"That was not kind!" said Hester. "When thou hast shown me a little love, thou mockest me!"

“那可不好！”赫斯特说。“你才对我表示了一些爱，就取笑我！”

"Why doth the minister sit yonder?" asked Pearl. 

“牧师为什么坐在那里？”珠儿问。

"He waits to welcome thee, " replied her mother. "Come thou, and entreat his blessing! He loves thee, my little Pearl, and loves thy mother too. Wilt thou not love him? Come! he longs to greet thee!"

“他等着欢迎你呢，”她母亲回答。“你过来，请求他的赐福吧！他爱你，我的小珠儿，他也爱妈妈。你难道不喜欢他吗？过来！他可想要问候你呢！”

"Doth he love us?" said Pearl, looking up with acute intelligence into her mother's face. "Will he go back with us, hand in hand, we three together, into the town?"

“他爱我们吗？”珠儿说，抬眼望着母亲的脸，敏锐而聪慧。“他会和我们手拉手，三个人一起回镇里去吗？”

"Not now, dear child, " answered Hester. "But in days to come he will walk hand in hand with us. We will have a home and fireside of our own; and thou shalt sit upon his knee; and he will teach thee many things, and love thee dearly. Thou wilt love him; wilt thou not?"

“现在不行，亲爱的宝贝。”赫斯特回答说。“但以后我们会手拉手走在一起的。我们会有自己的家和壁炉，你会坐在他膝盖上，他会教你很多东西，会深深地爱你。你会爱他的，不是吗？”

"And will he always keep his hand over his heart?" inquired Pearl. 

“他会经常把手捂在心口吗？”珠儿问道。

"Foolish child, what a question is that!" exclaimed her mother. "Come and ask his blessing!"

“傻孩子，这算什么问题啊！”她母亲大声说。“过来请求他的祈福！”

But, whether influenced by the jealousy that seems instinctive with every petted child towards a dangerous rival, or from whatever caprice of her freakish nature, Pearl would show no favour to the clergyman. It was only by an exertion of force that her mother brought her up to him, hanging back and manifesting her reluctance by odd grimaces; of which, ever since her babyhood, she had possessed a singular variety, and could transform her mobile physiognomy into a series of different aspects, with a new mischief in them, each and all. The minister — painfully embarrassed, but hoping that a kiss might prove a talisman to admit him into the child's kindlier regards — bent forward, and impressed one on her brow. Hereupon, Pearl broke away from her mother, and, running to the brook, stooped over it, and bathed her forehead, until the unwelcome kiss was quite washed off, and diffused through a long lapse of the gliding water. She then remained apart, silently watching Hester and the clergyman, while they talked together, and made such arrangements as were suggested by their new position, and the purposes soon to be fulfilled. 

不过，不知是出于每个宝贝孩子对危险对手本能的嫉妒，还是她那古灵精怪的天性，珠儿就是不喜欢牧师。她被妈妈硬拉着来到牧师跟前，还一个劲地往回缩，扮着奇怪的鬼脸以示不情愿。她自打婴儿时期起，就会做些独特的鬼脸，把那活泼的面孔变成不同的表情，每一个表情都带着新的恶作剧。牧师又是难过又是尴尬。他想，一个亲吻或许会起到奇异的效果，让他和孩子更亲近些，于是便弯腰向前，亲了下她的额头。珠儿一下挣脱她母亲，跑向小溪，俯下身子洗她的额头，直到那不受欢迎的亲吻完全洗干净了，扩散到那长长的流水之中。然后她远远地呆在一边，静静地看着赫斯特和牧师。他们正一起说着话，为新的情况和马上要去实行的目标做着各种安排。

And now this fateful interview had come to a close. The dell was to be left a solitude among its dark, old trees, which, with their multitudinous tongues, would whisper long of what had passed there, and no mortal be the wiser. And the melancholy brook would add this other tale to the mystery with which its little heart was already overburdened, and whereof it still kept up a murmuring babble, with not a whit more cheerfulness of tone than for ages heretofore. 

现在，这命运攸关的见面将近尾声。小山谷中留下的是一片孤寂，散落在这幽暗而古老的树木中。老树们七嘴八舌地窃窃私语了很久，谈论着这儿所发生的事情，没有人会比它们知道得更清楚。这忧郁的小溪，将在它那已经不堪重负的小小心灵中，再加上另一个神秘的故事。它依旧潺潺低语，音调不会比先前的那些世纪多半点欢快。

# Chapter 20THE MINISTER IN A MAZE

**第二十章 迷惘的牧师**

As the minister departed, in advance of Hester Prynne and little Pearl, he threw a backward glance, half expecting that he should discover only some faintly traced features or outline of the mother and the child slowly fading into the twilight of the woods. So great a vicissitude in his life could not at once be received as real. But there was Hester, clad in her grey robe, still standing beside the tree-trunk, which some blast had overthrown a long antiquity ago, and which time had ever since been covering with moss, so that these two fated ones, with earth's heaviest burden on them, might there sit down together, and find a single hour's rest and solace. And there was Pearl, too, lightly dancing from the margin of the brook — now that the intrusive third person was gone — and taking her old place by her mother's side. So the minister had not fallen asleep, and dreamed! 

牧师比赫斯特和小珠儿先走一步。他回头看了一眼，有些想再看看,那正渐渐消失于林子暮色中的母女俩隐约的形容。生活发生了如此巨大的变化，他一时间还无法相信。而赫斯特就在那里，穿着灰袍子，依旧站在树干的旁边；那树干是多年前被大风吹倒的，之后就开始长青苔，因此，那承受着世间极重的同命人能一起坐在上面，享受片刻的休憩与慰藉。还有珠儿——因为那闯入的第三者已经走了，她就轻快地从溪边跳到母亲身边她的老位置。这样看来，牧师刚才并没有睡着，也不是在做梦！

In order to free his mind from this indistinctness and duplicity of impression, which vexed it with a strange disquietude, he recalled and more thoroughly defined the plans which Hester and himself had sketched for their departure. It had been determined between them that the Old World, with its crowds and cities, offered them a more eligible shelter and concealment than the wilds of New England, or all America, with its alternatives of an Indian wigwam, or the few settlements of Europeans, scattered thinly along the seaboard. Not to speak of the clergyman's health, so inadequate to sustain the hardships of a forest life, his native gifts, his culture, and his entire development would secure him a home only in the midst of civilisation and refinement; the higher the state, the more delicately adapted to it the man. In furtherance of this choice, it so happened that a ship lay in the harbour; one of those questionable cruisers, frequent at that day, which, without being absolutely outlaws of the deep, yet roamed over its surface with a remarkable irresponsibility of character. This vessel had recently arrived from the Spanish Main, and, within three days' time, would sail for Bristol. Hester Prynne — whose vocation, as a self-enlisted Sister of Charity, had brought her acquainted with the captain and crew — could take upon herself to secure the passage of two individuals and a child, with all the secrecy which circumstances rendered more than desirable. 

为了摆脱那说不清道不明、搅得他莫名心烦的印象，他更加彻底地想了想和赫斯特做出的出走计划。他们达成了共识，认为那个旧世界人口稠密、城市密集，而在新英格兰或全美洲，那里的荒野上只疏落地散布着印第安人的茅屋，或是聚居着少数欧洲人，相比之下，前者能提供更好的隐居之所。不用说牧师那经不起林中艰苦生活的身体，就是他的心性和整个发展，也只能让他在文明的环境中安家。文明程度越高，他就更能适得其所。港口停泊着的一条船，使这个选择更有可行性。那是当时很普遍的一种形迹可疑的航船，虽不算是深海中的非法之徒，却以一种极不负责的姿态在海面游荡。这船刚从拉美北部海岸驶来，三天之内就要去往英国的布里斯托尔。作为妇女慈善会的志愿者，赫斯特·普林结识了船长和船员，并借此弄到了两个大人和一个孩子的舱位，拥有求之不得的私密空间。

The minister had inquired of Hester, with no little interest, the precise time at which the vessel might be expected to depart. It would probably be on the fourth day from the present. "That is most fortunate!" he had then said to himself. Now, why the Reverend Mr. Dimmesdale considered it so very fortunate, we hesitate to reveal. Nevertheless — to hold nothing back from the reader — it was because, on the third day from the present, he was to preach the Election Sermon; and as such an occasion formed an honourable epoch in the life of a New England clergyman, he could not have chanced upon a more suitable mode and time of terminating his professional career. "At least, they shall say of me, " thought this exemplary man, " hat I leave no public duty unperformed, nor ill performed!" Sad, indeed, that an introspection so profound and acute as this poor minister's should be so miserably deceived! We have had, and may still have, worse things to tell of him; but none, we apprehend, so pitiably weak; no evidence, at once so slight and irrefragable, of a subtle disease, that had long since begun to eat into the real substance of his character. No man, for any considerable period, can wear one face to himself, and another to the multitude, without finally getting bewildered as to which may be the true. 

牧师颇有兴致地问过赫斯特，船离港的确切时间。那船也许四天以后起航。“那太幸运了！”他当时这样自言自语。为什么丁梅斯代尔牧师先生觉得幸运呢，我们本不想说。不过，还是不隐瞒读者的好：三天后，他将要去庆祝选举的布道会上讲道。这是新英格兰牧师一生中的荣誉时刻，他再也找不到更好的方式和时机来结束自己的牧师生涯了。“至少，他们不会说我未尽公职，或草草了事！”这位模范牧师寻思着。实在是悲哀，这可怜的牧师如此深刻的反省，竟这样惨遭欺骗！我们已经讲过，也许还会讲到，有关他的一下更坏的事情，但就我们所知，再没有一件事能让他显得更软弱了，软弱得实在可怜。眼下，没有任何细小却不容置辩的迹象能说明，一种微妙的疾病早已开始蚕食他性格的本质了。没有人能长时间对自己扮出一副面孔，对众人又是另一副面孔，最后自己还搞得清哪一副面孔是真的。

The excitement of Mr. Dimmesdale's feelings, as he returned from his interview with Hester, lent him unaccustomed physical energy, and hurried him townward at a rapid pace. The pathway among the woods seemed wilder, more uncouth with its rude natural obstacles, and less trodden by the foot of man, than he remembered it on his outward journey. But he leaped across the plashy places, thrust himself through the clinging underbrush, climbed the ascent, plunged into the hollow, and overcame, in short, all the difficulties of the track, with an unweariable activity that astonished him. He could not but recall how feebly, and with what frequent pauses for breath, he had toiled over the same ground, only two days before. As he drew near the town, he took an impression of change from the series of familiar objects that presented themselves. It seemed not yesterday, not one, nor two, but many days, or even years ago, since he had quitted them. There, indeed, was each former trace of the street, as he remembered it, and all the peculiarities of the houses, with the due multitude of gable-peaks, and a weathercock at every point where his memory suggested one. Not the less, however, came this importunately obtrusive sense of change. The same was true as regarded the acquaintances whom he met, and all the well-known shapes of human life, about the little town. They looked neither older nor younger now; the beards of the aged were no whiter, nor could the creeping babe of yesterday walk on his feet today; it was impossible to describe in what respect they differed from the individuals on whom he had so recently bestowed a parting glance; and yet the minister's deepest sense seemed to inform him of their mutability. A similar impression struck him most remarkably, as he passed under the walls of his own church. The edifice had so very strange, and yet so familiar, an aspect, that Mr. Dimmesdale's mind vibrated between two ideas; either that he had seen it only in a dream hitherto, or that he was merely dreaming about it now. 

在和赫斯特会面后回来的路上，丁梅斯代尔先生情绪激动，体力变得非同寻常，步子飞快。比起记忆中来时的路，这林中的小道似乎更加荒蛮了，天然的高低不平令它显得更加坎坷，更加人迹罕至。但他跳过积水之地，穿过拉扯人的灌木，爬上高坡，步入低谷；总之，他用连他自己都惊讶的不懈精力，克服了路途上的一切障碍。他不禁想起，只在两天前的这同一片土地上，他是如此虚弱而疲惫地前行，还常常停下来喘气。他快到镇里的时候，那些熟悉的事物竟让他有种似是而非的印象。仿佛他不是昨天才离开这里的——不止一天，两天，而是好几天，甚至好几年了。那确实还是记忆中原先的街道。所有房屋的奇特之处，比如众多的山墙以及每个尖顶上的风信鸡，都和记忆里的一样。然而，这突兀的变迁之感依旧挥之不去。他碰到的人，还有人们生活的边边角角，也都没有变化。他们现在看上去没有变老，也没有变年轻。老人们的胡须没有更加花白，昨日还只会爬来爬去的婴儿，今天依旧不会走路。很难说那些他最近才看到过的人，到底在哪方面有了变化，但牧师最深处的意识却似乎告诉他：他们已经变了。他经过自己教堂的墙下时，那相似的印象给了他最深的感触。那高大的建筑显得如此陌生，却又如此熟悉；要么他曾在梦里见过，要么他现在就在梦里，丁梅斯代尔先生徘徊在这两种念头里。

This phenomenon, in the various shapes which it assumed, indicated no external change, but so sudden and important a change in the spectator of the familiar scene, that the intervening space of a single day had operated on his consciousness like the lapse of years. The minister's own will, and Hester's will, and the fate that grew between them, had wrought this transformation. It was the same town as heretofore; but the same minister returned not from the forest. He might have said to the friends who greeted him, "I am not the man for whom you take me! I left him yonder in the forest, withdrawn into a secret dell, by a mossy tree-trunk, and near a melancholy brook! Go seek your minister, and see if his emaciated figure, his thin cheek, his white, heavy, pain-wrinkled brow, be not flung down there, like a cast-off garment!" His friends, no doubt, would still have insisted with him — "Thou art thyself the man!" — but the error would have been their own, not his.
